The local simulation tracked a^k to 0.003 (step 7) because it draws substitutions independently. α fitted on this benchmark (1.5–2) is a check only: the shipped α must come from protein sequences, not from the benchmark.
  - **Mechanism 2 — several sample genes hit one unit.** 57–72% of units are also hit (DIAMOND, qcov ≥ 0.5, any rank) by present genes other than the gene whose nearest cluster they are. The benchmark's truth (`aai_truth`: depth-weighted identity of rank-1 genes only) ignores them. Scored against their union, predicted copies 1 − Π_g (1 − a_g^11), the 70–80% bias falls from +0.114 to +0.081, and at ≤ 70% from +0.227 to +0.149. So part of the "bias" is the truth's definition. The estimator also cannot separate variants: the unit-level EM splits k-mers shared between units, but a k-mer only unit X holds is credited wholly to X, even when the read came from a gene closer to unit Y.
  - **Mechanism 3 — selection at the detection limit.** A unit gets an `aai` only if some of its k-mers are hit. When the expected number of hit k-mers, `pin_sum` × (1 − e^(−coverage)) × S(a), is around 1 or less, the reported units are the lucky draws. At one hit k-mer, the zero-inflated fit caps present at 1, so `aai` = 1.0: 2,883 units, median truth 0.75. Bias by observed hit k-mers: 1 → +0.25, 2 → +0.17, 3 → +0.13, 5 → +0.09, 10+ → +0.03 (mechanism 1 included). This is what made the bias look depth-dependent in step 22: coverage enters through `seen`. A zero-truncated binomial MLE alone cuts the median bias at coverage ≤ 0.5 from +0.187 to +0.044, but leaves the high-coverage bias, which is mechanism 1. A per-sample empirical-Bayes posterior (NPMLE prior over identity) did no better.
  - **Design (agreed):**
    1. *Survival model in place of a^k.* S_k(a; α) as above, one α per (alphabet, k), recorded in the index's `meta.json` (e.g. `params`-independent `aai_model: {"survival": "regional_gamma", "alpha": ...}`, set at build or by `calibrate-aai`'s successor). With α → ∞ it is a^k, so an old index without the field behaves as now.
    2. *α from protein sequences, not reads.* Fit by least squares of observed survival against S(a; α), over protein pairs at known alignment identity spread over ~0.6–1.0. To match what the query measures, a pair is (protein P, MGnify90 cluster C). Survival = P's k-mers present in C's members' union ÷ `pin_sum` (C's average member's k-mers), against P's DIAMOND identity to C's representative. All k-mers count, not only kept ones, for precision; sampling is unbiased under the hash threshold. Use MGnify proteins from other clusters as P, against clusters of the 1-in-100 subset, which is not the benchmark's genomes. This is an HPC job (members live there). Ship it as a small Nextflow workflow per the HPC rule: sample ~10⁵ pairs, DIAMOND them, count k-mers with the Rust kernel. This also folds the union-mosaic effect (plan, Known biases 2) into α. Check that α is stable across identity bands, cluster sizes and biomes. If it is not, one parameter is not enough: consider α by `n_members` or `len_cv`.
    3. *Likelihood with selection.* Observed hit k-mers h (EM-attributed: present × m × seen) ~ Binomial(`pin_sum`, seen × S(a)), conditioned on h ≥ 1, the selection that puts a unit in the profile. The point estimate is the conditional MLE of a: solve E[h | h ≥ 1] = h, then invert S. The interval comes from the same likelihood (profile likelihood, or `aai_interval`'s delta terms with S'(a) in place of k a^(k−1)). Keep the existing extra variance from window clumping (`d`) and coverage (Fisher information on λ). The `min(1, copies)` cap goes: a ≤ 1 by construction.
    4. *Output and mask.* New column `aai_kmers` (h, the hit k-mers the estimate rests on, after the EM split). New query option `--min-aai-kmers` (default **5**): below it `aai`, `aai_lo` and `aai_hi` are null but `aai_kmers` is still reported. It replaces `MIN_AAI_KMERS` = 3 on *present* k-mers, which let single-hit units through. Reasons for 5: the SE of â is ≈ a/(k√h) before window clumping, i.e. ±0.07–0.09 at 95% with 5 k-mers, ±0.05 with 10 and ≥ ±0.1 at ≤ 3. On NovaSeq `tbase02_all`, 5 keeps ~55% of units that get an `aai` today; 8 would keep ~25%. The mask is applied after estimation, so it changes no estimate. `aai_naive` gets the same column and mask.
    5. *Benchmark changes (`bench.py`).* `aai_truth` gains a union truth over every present gene g hitting the unit: a_union = S⁻¹(1 − Π_g (1 − S(a_g))), the identity whose survival equals the union's. In the first version use S = a^k inside the union truth, so it does not depend on α. Report bias against both the nearest-gene and the union truth. `aai_score` reports bias and interval coverage also by `aai_kmers` bin (1–2, 3–4, 5–9, 10+), and interval coverage becomes the primary `aai` metric: truth-binned bias near the mask is selection that no per-unit estimator removes.
    6. *Fitted maps.* `aai-calibrate` / `calibrate-aai` (steps 16–17) stay for comparison but are no longer the plan of record. Step 22's coverage-binned map was written, then dropped unmerged.
  - **Decision rule for the new estimator** (rerun the four `floor` runs' profiles offline, from the tarball, and the `tiers` run when it reports): with α from protein pairs, bias against the union truth within ±0.02 at 70–95% for units with `aai_kmers` ≥ 5, in every run (read model and depth); interval coverage ≥ 0.9 per `aai_kmers` bin; no run-specific fitting. If it misses, the residual tells which mechanism is under-modelled.
  - **Deferred (measure first):** a read-level EM split (assign reads, not k-mers, to units, so X-only k-mers hit by a read that mostly hits Y do not count as X's present k-mers). It needs per-read hit rows, which the query keeps today only for the dense pass, the part being dropped (hundreds of millions of rows). The union truth bounds its gain: ~0.03 at 70–80% on this benchmark. Genuine mixtures of two variants nearest the same unit stay unresolvable at the unit level; the plan's multi-variant flag covers them.
  - **Order to build:** (1) the α workflow (HPC) and, meanwhile, items 3–5 with α as a parameter (tests on simulated pairs: an estimator under the regional-gamma model with known α recovers a; selection-conditioned MLE removes the bias of a 1–3-hit regime; the mask nulls `aai` but keeps `aai_kmers`); (2) rerun the four `floor` runs offline with the fitted α; (3) results and decision here.

* **Phase 7, step 24 — step 23's estimator built; the α workflow set up for HPC; a per-target α explored (code done, α fit and decision pending).**
  - **Built** (design items 1–5, with α as a parameter):
    - `query.survival`, `survival_identity` (closed-form inverse), `survival_slope`: S_k(a; α), and a^k when α is None.
    - `query.truncated_binomial_p`: the zero-truncated binomial MLE, n p / (1 − (1 − p)^n) = h, by bisection. It gives 0 at h ≤ 1, where the likelihood peaks at p → 0.
    - `query.aai_fit` replaces `aai` and `aai_interval`. h = present × m × seen; c = p̂ / seen, clipped to 1, then inverted through S. The interval keeps the three variance terms, with c in place of copies and S′(a) in place of k a^(k−1). The window-overlap factor d still uses independent substitutions, b = c^(1/k). The `min(1, copies)` cap is gone.
    - `aai_naive` uses the same survival model and truncated MLE on its own tier-2 hits (`kmers_hit`).
    - New column `aai_kmers` (h after the EM split; 0 for units gather drops). New query option `--min-aai-kmers` (default 5; `MIN_AAI_KMERS` was 3 on present k-mers). Below it `aai`, `aai_lo`, `aai_hi` and `aai_naive` are null, and `aai_kmers`/`kmers_hit` stay. The `MIN_AAI_LAMBDA` coverage guard stays. `--min-aai` > 0 now also drops units with a null `aai_naive`; at 0 nothing is dropped.
  - **Amendment: α is a sidecar, `aai_model.json`, not a `meta.json` field.** It is attached by `kmer-functional-profiler aai-model INDEX alpha.json`, checked against the index's k and alphabet, and loaded as `Index.aai_model`. This is the reason `aai_calibration.json` is a sidecar: `meta.json` and the hashes recorded of it stay as built. Without the file the query uses a^k, as before. Joint queries use the first index's α (same k and alphabet).
  - **Benchmark (`bench.py`).**
    - `union_truth`: per unit, the a^k-survival union over every present gene hitting it (qcov ≥ 0.5, any rank), as `true_union`.
    - `aai_score` adds `aai_bias_union_<lo>`, `aai_cover_union_<lo>` and `aai_cover_union` (the primary `aai` metric), plus `aai_bias_kmers<lo>`, `aai_cover_kmers<lo>` and `aai_n_kmers<lo>` by `aai_kmers` bin (0–2, 3–4, 5–9, 10+; the first bin starts at 0 because h is fractional).
    - `reestimate_aai` recomputes the four columns from an archived profile's zero-inflated fit. It recovers pin_sum = present × m / `copies_zi` and the clumping from `hits`/`reads`. A test checks that it reproduces the query exactly. `aai-score --alpha A --min-aai-kmers N` applies it, which is decision step (2): the step-20 tarball's `units/*.tsv` have every column it needs.
  - **α workflow: `workflows/aai-alpha`** (Nextflow, Slurm + Singularity; `hpc/kfp-ablations/aai-alpha/run.sh` over the 1-in-100 subset):
    - QUERIES: 2×10⁵ random members as P.
    - DIAMOND against the cluster representatives (`--more-sensitive`, identity ≥ 55%, both coverages ≥ 80%).
    - PAIRS: up to 5,000 pairs per 0.02 of identity over 0.6–1.0.
    - SURVIVAL: every k-mer via `_core.hash_proteins`; P is left out of C when it is a member (leave-one-out).
    - FIT: `alpha.json`, plus `alpha_strata.tsv` by identity band and cluster size.
    - Choices and limits:
      - P comes from the subset's own members, in other clusters or left out of its own, rather than from the release outside the subset. The subset is not the benchmark's genomes, and the leave-one-out pairs supply the 0.9–1.0 band.
      - Both coverages ≥ 0.8, so partial overlaps do not enter α. The benchmark's truth uses qcov ≥ 0.5, so length mismatch remains there.
      - No biome stratum, because the members table has none.
      - No P_IN floor in pin_sum.
    - Tested on synthetic members and with a `-stub` run. **Pending: the HPC run.**
  - **Explored: one α per target (unit), with a prior.** The user's question: if the excess survival comes from clustered conserved k-mers, model it per target, shrunk to a prior when a target has little evidence. Does that work, and does it help abundance too? It was answered by a scratch simulation. Each family has a site-rate map and members at chosen depths; k = 11, L = 400; substitutions only, star tree. Results:
    - *Spatial clustering is the cause, not rate variation as such.* iid site rates, even gamma shape 0.2, give survival = a^k exactly (fitted α → ∞, RMSE equal to a^k's). Rates shared over regions of ~10–100 aa give α 0.7–1.7, and the fit holds across bands: α at ≥ 0.9 and at < 0.85 agree within ~0.1. So α measures conservation clustered at the k-mer scale. It is not the phylogenetic Γ shape (Yang), whose published per-family values therefore cannot serve as priors.
    - *α is a property of the family.* Under one generating process, per-family α ranges 0.78 / 1.41 / 3.46 (10/50/90%), because each family has its own map of conserved regions. A per-target α is meaningful.
    - *Estimable from a cluster's own members at build time, if identities are known.* α fitted on n members at 90–97% to the rep predicts the family's survival for queries at 65–90%. The aai error's 90% |err| goes from 0.12–0.13 with the population α to 0.085–0.09 with the family's own α at n ≥ 10 (regions ~30 aa). At n = 3 a log-scale shrinkage halfway to the population α does best (0.098 vs 0.110 own, 0.121 population). Bias is ~0 for every α choice. The global α removes the bias (+0.06 under a^k); per-target α only tightens per-unit error, by ~25–35% at ≥ 10 members.
    - *What it needs.* MGnify's cluster tables do not record member-to-rep identity (`mgy_cluster_seqs` has rep and member only), so the build would need alignments of members to their representative. An alignment-free estimate from `pin_hist` dispersion (k-mers conserved across all members versus few) is confounded by shared ancestry among members, which also over-disperses p_in. Most MGnify90 clusters are singletons or small, so for most units α would be the prior. A middle level that needs no build-time alignment is α per Pfam family, fitted by the α workflow (it has the subset's `pfam.parquet`): global → Pfam → unit.
    - *At query time.* α cannot be estimated per unit from a sample: a unit gives one count h, and a and α are confounded in S(a; α). Applying a per-unit α is free: `aai_fit` is elementwise, so α can be an array read with the hit units' rows. Stored as a log-quantised u8 column it is ~1 byte per unit.
    - *Abundance.* Little effect on point estimates. `coverage_em`/`coverage_zi` are rates per present k-mer: the zero-inflated fit absorbs which k-mers are missing, however they cluster. `copies_zi` conflates divergence with copy number, and α does not separate them from one count. It can improve intervals: clustered survival over-disperses present k-mers beyond the independent-window factor d, the likely reason the posterior needed the `COPIES_ERROR` floor (step 18). It also changes the census's reading: census k-mers are hit at ~70% identity (S(0.7; 1.5) ≈ 0.13, against a^k = 0.02), not only at ≥ 80%.
    - *Recommendation.* First check that between-family variation is real in MGnify, from the α run: add per-cluster or per-Pfam α with its sampling error, and compare their spread with the noise. If it is real, ship α per Pfam (global prior for units without one) before per-unit α from build-time alignments. Scratch only; the simulation is described above.
  - **Order:** (1) α HPC run; (2) `aai-score --alpha` over the four `floor` runs' archived profiles; (3) results and decision rule here; (4) if strata or the per-Pfam spread show α varies, per-Pfam α.

* **Phase 7, step 25 — a generative survival model (Markov-modulated rates) and a grounded estimator (code on `dev-aai`, not main until the ablations are done; protein-pair fit pending).** Asked whether step 24's model is grounded in the system's probabilities: only partly. The gamma-window formula is the long-region limit of a consistent model; α mixed rate contrast with region length; the overlap factor d assumed independent substitutions; the truncated binomial assumed independent windows.
  - **Model (`survival.py`, `SurvivalModel`).** Site rates from `categories` (16) equiprobable gamma categories of shape `shape`, mean 1, redrawn from the stationary distribution with probability 1/`region` per site: a Markov chain along the sequence. Exact identity a(t), window survival S(t) and co-survival B_j(t) of windows j apart follow from 16-state transfer products, tabulated once on a grid of divergence t (1,200 rows, lags to 300; 13 ms per model). `shape` None is independent substitutions (a^k, and d as before to 4 digits). iid site rates (`region` 1) give a^k whatever the shape: only clustered conservation raises survival.
    - Checked against Monte Carlo of the same process, nothing fitted: survival within 0.01, window correlation at lags 1, 10, 40 within 0.03 (test). The step-24 gamma-window formula with the true shape was off by up to 0.055 (regions ~10 aa).
  - **Estimator (`query.survival_quantiles`), replacing the delta interval and the truncated binomial.** At each grid row the hit count h = Σ x_i y_i (x_i survival, correlated; y_i hit, Bernoulli(seen) in read clumps) is a count with mean n seen S and variance n seen S (1 − seen) + n seen² S (1 − S) d + (clumping − 1) n S² seen (1 − seen), n = `pin_sum`, d from the model's correlation sums for the unit's window count (`len_mean` − k + 1) and sampling rate (`unit_windows`). The count is binomial or negative binomial matched to those moments (`count_cdf`, closed form via incomplete beta/gamma). It is conditioned on the selection the estimate is reported under: h ≥ `--min-aai-kmers` for `aai` (h ≥ 1 if 0). The point is the median-unbiased (mid-p) estimate; the bounds a Neyman interval; each a bisection over the grid's rows. Coverage uncertainty is parameter uncertainty, not scatter in h: the bounds take seen at the ends of its interval. `aai_naive` uses the same routine (with read clumping, so it equals `aai` without shared k-mers). Cost: ~6.5 s per archived NovaSeq profile (5.6 × 10⁵ units), ~29 s per 10⁶ units with an estimate.
    - Dead ends on the way, kept for the record: a delta interval held 54% at 74% identity with 100-aa regions; a Beta on survival collapsed estimates at low coverage, because survival h / (n seen) is unbounded there; the `n/d` clump zero mass pulled strains with up to ~70 hit windows to identity 0; adding the coverage-estimate variance to h and then conditioning on h ≥ 5 over-corrected.
    - Simulation (k = 11, 400 aa, coverage 20): coverage of the true identity 0.89–0.97 for shape 0.5–1, regions 30–100 aa, identity 0.73–0.95 (strain's own identity 0.95–1.0). At low coverage (seen 0.05–0.6), units with h ≥ 5 estimated given that selection: medians 0.707, 0.788, 0.798, 0.896, 0.901, 0.899 for truths 0.7, 0.8, 0.8, 0.9, 0.9, 0.9 (inverting the hits of the same units read up to +0.02 at 0.9, +0.1 at 0.8), coverage ≥ 0.99. One hit k-mer gives [0, 1].
  - **Fit workflow: `workflows/aai-model` (was `aai-alpha`; `hpc/kfp-ablations/aai-model/run.sh`, check out `dev-aai` on HPC).** As step 24's, plus co-survival: per pair, P's windows in order (P drawn from members of standard residues only, so emission order is window position) give `window_survival` and `both_<j>` for j in 1, 3, 6, 11, 20, 40, 80, 150. FIT: least squares of survival and co-survival over (log shape, log region), three starts; `aai_model.json` (`survival` "markov_gamma", `shape`, `region`, `categories`, `k`, `alphabet`, `fit`) and `model_strata.tsv`. Recovery on pairs simulated from the model: (1.0, 30) → (1.16, 28); (0.5, 10) → (0.45, 7.6); (2.0, 100) → (3.2, 54): weak contrast over long regions is poorly identified, though its predictions fit.
  - **Offline check on archived profiles** (step-20 tarball, `floor` NovaSeq and 1.65 M reads, `tbase02_all`, seeds 1–3, means; `aai-score` metrics against the union truth). A: as archived (a^k, present ≥ 3); B: the new estimator under a^k, mask 5; C: B under the Markov model with the shape fitted on this benchmark's survival-vs-identity curve (seed-1 NovaSeq units hit by one gene, coverage > 4), for regions 10, 30, 100: shapes 0.96, 1.32, 1.47, all RMSE 0.043 against a^k's 0.119. Survival alone cannot tell the regions apart, as expected. **C is a check, not a result: its shape comes from the benchmark.**

    | run | arm | units | bias 95+ | 90–95 | 80–90 | 70–80 | 50–70 | cover all | 90–95 | 80–90 | 70–80 |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | NovaSeq | A, all units | 8,862 | −0.004 | +0.013 | +0.050 | +0.118 | +0.227 | 0.71 | 0.83 | 0.78 | 0.49 |
    | NovaSeq | A, B's units | 4,011 | −0.003 | +0.016 | +0.057 | +0.133 | +0.254 | 0.68 | 0.78 | 0.62 | 0.09 |
    | NovaSeq | B (a^k) | 4,011 | −0.007 | +0.007 | +0.034 | +0.087 | +0.152 | 0.84 | 0.88 | 0.90 | 0.86 |
    | NovaSeq | C, region 10 | 4,011 | −0.009 | −0.010 | −0.014 | −0.035 | −0.210 | 0.88 | 0.89 | 0.95 | 0.96 |
    | NovaSeq | C, region 30 | 4,011 | −0.009 | −0.011 | −0.029 | −0.151 | −0.354 | 0.89 | 0.92 | 0.97 | 0.98 |
    | 1.65 M | A, B's units | 2,053 | 0.000 | +0.026 | +0.076 | +0.156 | +0.268 | 0.80 | 0.92 | 0.73 | 0.18 |
    | 1.65 M | B (a^k) | 2,053 | −0.008 | +0.006 | +0.045 | +0.100 | +0.182 | 0.92 | 0.94 | 0.92 | 0.90 |
    | 1.65 M | C, region 10 | 2,053 | −0.011 | −0.010 | +0.005 | +0.003 | +0.005 | 0.95 | 0.95 | 0.96 | 0.96 |
    | 1.65 M | C, region 30 | 2,053 | −0.012 | −0.013 | −0.008 | −0.089 | −0.158 | 0.96 | 0.96 | 0.98 | 0.98 |

    - Interpretation. On the same units, the selection-conditioned count-space estimator alone (B) cuts the 70–80% bias by about a third (+0.13 → +0.09 NovaSeq, +0.16 → +0.10 at 1.65 M) and lifts interval coverage from 0.68–0.80 to 0.84–0.92 overall, and at 70–80% from 0.09–0.18 to 0.86–0.90. The rest of the bias is survival above a^k: with short regions (C, region 10) it is within ±0.015 at 80–100% in both runs and at 70–80% at 1.65 M (−0.035 NovaSeq), and NovaSeq and 1.65 M now agree within ~0.03, which step 22's per-depth calibration could not achieve. Longer regions over-correct below 80%: the region length matters, which is why the protein-pair fit measures co-survival. Spearman is unchanged (0.40–0.47), as expected for a monotone change. The mask at 5 halves the units with an `aai` (8,862 → 4,011 NovaSeq).
  - **Order:** (1) the `aai-model` HPC run from `dev-aai`; (2) `aai-score --model results/aai_model.json` over the `floor` and `tiers` profiles; (3) decision rule of step 23 against the union truth; (4) merge `dev-aai` into main after the ablations.

* **Phase 7, step 26 — sylph's read-error estimate tested against the Phred-based one: not adopted (`dev-aai`).** Sylph uses no error estimate for ANI: its zero-inflated fit absorbs the uniform thinning errors cause, as `aai` does here. It estimates the error survival E only to turn effective coverage into depth for `--estimate-unknown`: E = 1 − n₁ / Σ_{a>1} a nₐ over the read sketch's k-mer multiplicities (singletons taken as errors; the paper advises giving ε for soil and ocean). Here, `sample_summary`'s `error_thinning` plays that role, from Phred and masked bases.
  - **Test (local, scratch).** Eight bacterial genomes (38 Mb, 332 contigs), InSilicoSeq lognormal abundances, seed 1, 2 M reads: error-free at 151 and 301 bp (patched perfect model), NovaSeq (151 bp) and MiSeq (301 bp); all pairs, 1 in 4 and 1 in 20. Index: the genomes' six-frame ORFs ≥ 100 aa (88,895 units), `t_base` 0.02. True thinning: the summed `coverage_zi` of units at ≥ 3× (error-free), error set over the error-free set of the same read length. Read length alone changes depth (a 301-bp read more often runs past a reading frame's end, which drops the frame), hence the 301-bp error-free set. E is on all sampled read k-mers and on the 1-in-256 subsample the query keeps.

    | reads | model | true | Phred `error_thinning` | sylph E (all) | sylph E (1/256) |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | all | error-free 151 | 1 | 0.981 | 0.980 | 0.982 |
    | all | NovaSeq | 0.950 | 0.939 | 0.943 | 0.934 |
    | all | error-free 301 | 1 | 0.971 | 0.992 | 0.993 |
    | all | MiSeq | 0.786 | 0.745 | 0.854 | 0.849 |
    | 1 in 4 | NovaSeq | 0.935 | 0.940 | 0.853 | 0.828 |
    | 1 in 4 | MiSeq | 0.773 | 0.745 | 0.778 | 0.745 |
    | 1 in 4 | error-free 151 | 1 | 0.981 | 0.901 | 0.911 |
    | 1 in 20 | NovaSeq | 0.913 | 0.940 | 0.503 | 0.419 |
    | 1 in 20 | MiSeq | 0.748 | 0.744 | 0.504 | 0.510 |
    | 1 in 20 | error-free 151 | 1 | 0.981 | 0.582 | 0.639 |

  - **Interpretation.** The Phred estimate is within 0.041 of the truth everywhere and does not depend on depth. Sylph's E matches it only at full depth (NovaSeq −0.007 against Phred −0.011; MiSeq +0.068 against −0.041). It falls with depth even for error-free reads (0.98 → 0.90 → 0.58), because k-mers of low-coverage contigs occur once: its assumption 1. A real metagenome has far more low-coverage sequence than this sample, so E would be worse there. It also misses errors that drop whole frames (a stop gains no singleton k-mers). Not added. The one thing it shows: the Phred estimate reads 0.97–0.98 for error-free reads (masked bases and nominal qualities), a floor of ~2–3% thinning that is not errors; small, left as is.
* **Phase 7, step 27 — second ablation export: the `tiers` run, the rescored `floor` and `host` runs, `floor` error-free (results; no decision taken).** `results/ablations-v2`: the export minus `units/` and `mgnify/` (22 GB, left in the tarball; `results/ablations-v1` deleted, its `index` run moved here because the export's `index` is a failed re-run, kept as `trace.failed-rerun.tsv`). Runs: `tiers` NovaSeq and query cost at main 73f31d3; the four `floor` runs and both `reads` runs re-profiled with `-resume` at main 157a06d (PROFILE re-ran, as the benchmark code changed); `floor` error-free and `host` (full re-run, decoy units no longer scored) at dcc4061. Numbers are means over 10 seeds; MGnify90 detection at `kmers_unique` ≥ 1 unless stated; Pfam at the MGnify90 level summed per Pfam, `coverage_em`.
  - **Step 20's numbers moved slightly under the newer code.** Every arm's `completeness_90` is ~0.02 lower (NovaSeq sparse 0.337 → 0.315, `tbase02_all` 0.654 → 0.632); `aai` bias within 0.002 and Spearman within 0.003 of step 20; Pfam within 0.012. Orderings and every step-20 conclusion hold, including read QC (fastp = raw; *q* 20/30 cost completeness) and frame mode (`edges30` best on L1). The step-20 corrupt `dense02` profile did not recur; `dense02` now calibrates within ±0.01 in all five runs. One residual oddity: `floor-26.4M` seed 7 edges20, where `sparse` and `dense02` (identical pass 1) differ by 0.004 `completeness_90` and 1,171 genes with a hit; `dense02~edges20` was OOM-killed in 10 of 11 seeds before a retry passed.
  - **Index size and query cost at 1 in 100** (`du` GB; ERR7738575 + ERR7746321 pooled, wall s / peak anonymous GB at 100 M pairs; ×100 for today's full-scale format, step 21's estimate in brackets):

    | Index | GB | Full scale | 100 M pairs |
    | --- | --- | --- | --- |
    | sparse | 0.77 | 77 | 203 / 1.1 |
    | `slim` | 0.91 | 91 (~91) | 202 / 1.2 |
    | `tbase01` | 1.47 | 147 | 226 / 1.5 |
    | `dense02` (step 20) | 1.99 | 199 | 479 / 54 |
    | `tbase02_n16` | 2.01 | 201 (~240) | 222 / 1.8 |
    | `tbase02_all` | 2.22 | 222 | 231 / 1.9 |
    | `tbase05` (step 20) | 2.58 | 258 | 254 / 2.5 |
    | `tbase05_all` | 2.82 | 282 (~310) | 320 / 2.9 |
    | `tbase10_all` | 3.71 | 371 (~430) | **2,029** / 4.9 |

    `tbase10_all` at 100 M pairs spent 1,777 s in the EM: its largest component (10.9 M pairs) took the blocked path and 13,410 block steps to converge, against ≤ 1,000 everywhere else (210 s at 40 M pairs). This is the ceiling the `ponytail:` note in `_fit_blocked` names (blocks are runs of unit ids, not a graph partition). At ≤ 40 M pairs every tier is ≤ 210 s and ≤ 4 GB.
  - **`aai` (tiers, plain, own-run calibration on held-out clusters).** Raw bias at 90 / 80 / 70% and Spearman: `tbase02_all` +0.038 / +0.074 / +0.136, 0.433; `tbase02_n16` +0.042 / +0.088 / +0.166, 0.259; `tbase05_all` +0.024 / +0.062 / +0.127, 0.473; `tbase10_all` +0.015 / +0.048 / +0.105, 0.562 (`aai_n` 8.5 k, 6.4 k, 11.2 k, 15.2 k). Sparse, `slim` and `tbase01` have no usable `aai` (Spearman −0.19 to −0.01) and do not calibrate (+0.16 to +0.21 at 70%). Calibrated: `tbase02_all`, `tbase05_all` and `tbase10_all` within ±0.004 at 70–95%, coverage 0.94–0.97; **`tbase02_n16` misses at 70% (−0.051; seeds −0.066 to +0.032)**: like `tbase02`, keeping a best-by-score subset biases `aai` per unit. Across the five `floor` runs (own maps), `tbase05` (plain) is within ±0.005 everywhere, `tbase02_all` everywhere but 1.65 M reads at 70% (−0.034; step 20 had it within ±0.01). Transfer between runs is step 22's problem and step 25's estimator replaces these maps; the `aai-model` fit has not run, so step 23's decision rule is still pending.
  - **MGnify90 detection at matched thresholds** (offline: `units/` streamed from the tarball and scored as `aai_score` does at `kmers_unique` ≥ 1–5; at 1 it reproduces the HPC scores exactly). `completeness_90` / `purity_nearest` / `purity_near`, NovaSeq:

    | Index | ≥ 1 | ≥ 2 | ≥ 3 | ≥ 4 |

    | sparse | 0.315 / 0.153 / 0.328 | 0.223 / 0.207 / 0.337 | 0.166 / 0.214 / 0.322 | 0.120 / 0.214 / 0.313 |
    | `slim` | 0.328 / 0.150 / 0.363 | 0.250 / 0.227 / 0.418 | 0.201 / 0.252 / 0.413 | 0.160 / 0.264 / 0.409 |
    | `tbase01` | 0.533 / 0.142 / 0.330 | 0.401 / 0.224 / 0.409 | 0.294 / 0.260 / 0.428 | 0.210 / 0.280 / 0.434 |
    | `tbase02` | 0.622 / 0.137 / 0.331 | 0.518 / 0.229 / 0.441 | 0.426 / 0.286 / 0.495 | 0.340 / 0.322 / 0.526 |
    | `tbase02_n16` | 0.638 / 0.121 / 0.309 | 0.548 / 0.196 / 0.391 | 0.466 / 0.242 / 0.429 | 0.389 / 0.270 / 0.448 |
    | `tbase02_all` | 0.632 / 0.099 / 0.301 | 0.554 / 0.154 / 0.379 | 0.486 / 0.190 / 0.412 | 0.422 / 0.216 / 0.429 |
    | `tbase05` | 0.723 / 0.129 / 0.330 | 0.658 / 0.220 / 0.457 | 0.603 / 0.284 / 0.526 | 0.553 / 0.329 / 0.569 |

    At ≥ 2 every floor except `slim` beats sparse-at-1 on all three. The `_all` arms buy completeness with purity: units per sample at ≥ 1 are 47 k sparse, 96 k `tbase02`, 145 k `tbase05`, 194 k `tbase02_all`, 214 k `tbase05_all`, 242 k `tbase10_all`. At matched `purity_nearest` ≈ 0.22, `completeness_90` is `tbase10_all` 0.707 (≥ 3), `tbase05` 0.658 (≥ 2), `tbase05_all` 0.626 (≥ 3), `tbase02` 0.518 (≥ 2), `tbase02_n16` ~0.48, `tbase02_all` 0.422 (≥ 4), `tbase01` 0.401 (≥ 2), sparse 0.214 (≥ 3). So plain `tbase05` beats `tbase05_all` here at 9% less disk; `_all` only buys `aai` (Spearman 0.424 → 0.473, `aai_n` +12%).
  - **Pfam (MGnify90 units summed per Pfam), against sparse at 1** (Δ purity / completeness / Spearman / L1): at `min_hits` 3, `tbase02_all` (+0.001 to +0.006 / +0.032 to +0.048 / +0.046 to +0.063 / −0.03 to −0.05) and `tbase05` (+0.010 to +0.014 / +0.030 to +0.040 / +0.054 to +0.074 / −0.056 to −0.081) **pass step 19's rule 3 in all five `floor` runs**, as `tbase02` does at 2. Tiers NovaSeq at 3: `tbase02_n16` 0.781 / 0.738 / 0.842 / 0.496 (at 2: 0.743 / 0.778, sparse's purity); `tbase05_all` 0.730 / 0.800 and `tbase10_all` 0.704 / 0.825 still miss purity at 3 (−0.013, −0.039), so they need ≥ 4. `slim` at 1: 0.716 / 0.777 / 0.815 / 0.526; at sparse-at-1's purity (0.743, interpolated) completeness 0.752 against sparse's 0.742 and `tbase01`'s 0.759: 60% of `tbase01`'s gain for 20% of its extra disk.
  - **Host (rescored, KO; FP per sample / purity / completeness at 0 / 50 / 90 / 99% host).** The decoy arms are valid now and do almost nothing: base raw 111 / 104 / 82 / 130, decoy 111 / 103 / 81 / 128, mask 110 / 89 / 47 / 17, hostile 111 / 89 / 46 / 14; `mask_decoy` equals mask in every cell, and `tbase02` and Pfam show the same. The decoy absorbs ≤ 4 of the host-caused false positives; the mask removes them (why the decoy misses them is not measured here). `host_like_detected` is 0 in every mask arm. `tbase02`'s rows match step 20's (raw 191 / 172 / 134 / 204, mask 189 / 157 / 96 / 61). Step 20's conclusions stand: ship the mask; the decoy role is not a host filter. `tbase02_all`, `tbase05` and the tiers are still not in `host` (step 21, follow-up 4).
  - **What this means for step 21's tiers (for the user to decide):**
    - *Slim:* `slim` is +18% disk over sparse for a small gain: MGnify90 detection at ≥ 1 is +0.013 completeness and −0.003 `purity_nearest`, ahead on all three at ≥ 2; Pfam completeness +0.01 at matched purity. `tbase01` (+90% disk) gives the floor's detection gain. If slim has to be ~60–90 GB, `slim` is the only candidate; otherwise sparse is as good for function-level output.
    - *Standard:* `tbase02_all` (222 GB today's format) is the only ~200 GB arm that calibrates; `tbase02_n16` (201 GB) has better unit purity but does not. If containment `aai` ships without fitted maps (step 25), the comparison is on step 23's rule, not calibration, and needs re-running on these profiles.
    - *Large:* plain `tbase05` (258 GB) dominates `tbase05_all` (282 GB) on detection and Pfam and calibrates in every run; `tbase10_all` (371 GB) is best on every accuracy metric (`aai` Spearman 0.56, bias at 70% +0.105) but needs ≥ 4 hits for Pfam purity and the EM fix for queries over ~40 M pairs.
  - **Next:** (1) partition the blocked EM by the unit graph (or raise `MAX_FIT_PAIRS`), then re-time `tbase10_all` at 100 M pairs; (2) the `aai-model` HPC fit, then `aai-score --model` on the `floor` and `tiers` profiles (stream `units/` from the tarball, as here); (3) `host` with MGnify-unit tiers; (4) Pfam at `min_hits` 4–5 for the `_all` tiers.

* **Phase 7, step 28 — the standard tier at ~120 GB, and frame mode on the release tiers (set up on main, not run).** Slim and large are decided (project-wide, 2026-10-06).
  - **Standard candidates** (`ablations/standard.config`; `hpc/kfp-ablations/standard-novaseq`, then `standard-query-cost`). As `tiers`, NovaSeq, `min_hits` 1–4, with `sparse` and `tbase02_all` as controls:
    - `tbase02_n16` (`--t-base 0.02 --n-min 16 --oversample 2`): measured in step 27 at 2.01 GB at 1 in 100, ~120 GB compact. Its `aai` missed calibration at 70% (−0.051); re-run here so it is scored on the same run as the others.
    - `tbase015_all` (`--t-base 0.015 --n-min 32 --oversample 1`): `tbase02_all`'s floor, the base stratum at 0.015. Estimated ~2.0 GB at 1 in 100 (`tbase01` → `tbase02` added 0.39 GB per 0.01 of *t\_base*). The lower base stratum costs singletons (73% of clusters), which carry most of the floor's MGnify90 detection (`slim`, step 27).
    - `tbase02_all16` (`--t-base 0.02 --n-min 16 --oversample 1`): every candidate up to half `tbase02_all`'s *t\_g*, so about as many k-mers as `tbase02_n16` (~120 GB compact), but sampled by hash, not chosen by score. Units with more than 16 candidates by chance are cut to their best 16, as `tbase02_all` cuts at 32, which calibrated. Added beyond the two the user named, as the unbiased twin of `tbase02_n16`.
    - Sizes at full scale carry step 21's ±20–30% until the whole-release statistics report; "compact" is not built yet.
    - *Run from main.* The selection-conditioned `aai` estimate (steps 24–25, on `dev-aai`) is applied offline: `dev-aai`'s `aai-score --model` re-estimates `aai` from the archived profiles once the `aai-model` fit has run, as for the `floor` runs. *Choice:* the arm at ~120 GB with the best `aai` on step 23's rule (against the union truth, by `aai_kmers` bin), then detection at matched purity and Pfam against sparse (step 19's rules 2–3 at matched thresholds).
  - **Frame mode: what the existing runs say.** The rule (project-wide, 2026-10-01): change the default from stop-free only if edges (or all) cuts the short-protein abundance error without losing purity, at an acceptable hashing cost.
    - *Function level (`reads`, KO and Pfam units, `min_hits` 1).* Edges and all recover completeness: Pfam on `pfam_tbase02` +0.009 NovaSeq, +0.031 MiSeq; KO on base +0.024 / +0.066. Purity falls 0.001–0.007 (NovaSeq) and 0.006–0.019 (MiSeq). All is worse than edges on purity (MiSeq KO base 0.957 against 0.963) and L1 (`tbase02` NovaSeq 0.204 against 0.171), for no extra completeness. `edges30` has the best L1 and purity of the three.
    - *MGnify90 level (`floor`, edges20 against stop-free, five runs).* `completeness_90` +0.003 to +0.064 (largest on MiSeq and on floors), `purity_nearest` −0.003 to −0.017. Pfam (MGnify90 units summed): purity −0.011 to −0.018 (MiSeq −0.040 to −0.043) for completeness +0.004 to +0.032 and L1 −0.007 to −0.051. The purity loss at `min_hits` 1 is larger than any floor-arm difference that rule 3 allows, but it may be the threshold shift step 20 found for floors (more hits per unit). Calibrated `aai` is unchanged (±0.006).
    - *Cost.* Profile time (`reads`, median per profile): edges20 +15% (NovaSeq) to +22% (MiSeq), all +28–42%. No query-cost run on real reads per frame mode.
    - *Missing:* the rule's abundance error by protein length (the benchmark had none); purity at matched thresholds; all six frames at the MGnify90 level; the release-tier indexes; time on a real sample. **No decision yet.**
  - **Frame-mode run** (`ablations/frames.config`; `hpc/kfp-ablations/frames-novaseq`, `frames-miseq`, then `frames-query-cost`): `slim`, `tbase02_all` (standing in for the standard tier) and `tbase05_all`; frames stop-free, edges20, edges30, all; `min_hits` 1–4; once per read model. 120 profiles per run.
    - *`aai_score` by gene length* (`LENGTH_BINS`: < 150, 150–300, 300–600, ≥ 600 aa; length = bases / depth / 3 from the truth): `completeness_90_len<lo>` (present genes in their cluster at the 90% level whose cluster is detected), and `abund_bias_len<lo>` / `abund_err_len<lo>`: median and median absolute log2 error of `coverage_em` / depth, after removing the sample's median log2 ratio, for units that are one present gene's 90%-level cluster and nearest to no other gene. This is step 7's `bias_len_*` on the fmh benchmark. Test: `test_aai_score_by_gene_length`.
    - *Query cost per frame mode:* `mgnify-subset --query_args` adds options to every query cell; `frames-query-cost` runs the ladder (4 M, 40 M, 100 M pooled pairs) once per mode, into `results-<mode>/`.
    - *Decision:* edges20 or edges30 becomes the default if, on NovaSeq and MiSeq, it lowers `abund_err_len0`/`len150` and raises `completeness_90_len0`/`len150`, without losing Pfam or MGnify90 purity at matched thresholds, at ≤ 25% more query time at 100 M pairs. All only if it beats edges on those. Otherwise stop-free stays.
  - *Checked:* stub runs of `standard` and `frames` on the test profile (with a laptop cap on `MGNIFY_*` resources) give 10 and 24 profiles over 2 seeds, a calibration map per profile and every index published.

* **Phase 7, step 29 — EM convergence: why fits stopped at `max_iter`, and the block-wise fit made accurate (code; HPC re-timing pending).** Step 27 found the EM at its 1,000-step cap in almost every query-cost cell (0–180 units unconverged), 43,696 unconverged units in `tbase01` at 40 M pairs (its largest component, 43,448 units), and `tbase10_all` at 100 M pairs spending 1,777 s in the EM (447 block rounds; the only component in any run above `MAX_FIT_PAIRS`, 10.9 M pairs). The counters cover the plain EM (`coverage_em`) only.
  - **Cause (synthetic, scratch).** 31 of 3,692 random low-coverage components (units on a shared k-mer pool, 40% present, lognormal coverage) stop at the cap. In all 31 the unit still moving holds no k-mer of its own among its hits: coverage drifts between units sharing all their hit k-mers, along a nearly flat likelihood, and EM is very slow there. Not the boundary at 0 (4 of 31 go there) nor large components (a 300-unit chain converges in 24 steps). Each converges in 1,014–5,523 steps. At the cap the worst coverage error is 0.009 (≤ 1% of the component's largest) and the log-likelihood is within 3 × 10⁻⁴ of the optimum: **the cap costs no accuracy**; `em_unconverged_units` flags flat directions, not errors. The test is per component, so one such unit keeps its whole component iterating (the 43,696).
  - **Rejected: a log-likelihood stopping test.** Stopping a plain-EM component when its log-likelihood changes by ≤ *tol* × its magnitude saves steps on the ridges (median 234 against the 1,000 cap) but slow directions are not only ridges: on the test families the coverage error rises from 7 × 10⁻⁶ (parameter test) to 4 × 10⁻³ at *tol* 1e-8 and 4 × 10⁻⁴ at 1e-10, for 1.2–2.3× fewer steps. The zero-inflated and *p\_in* updates have no closed-form objective with shared k-mers, and a test on the hit k-mers' means alone stops them halfway along the coverage–presence trade-off (0.92 against 2.82). The parameter test stays.
  - **Rejected: a round budget for block-wise fits** (rounds ≤ `max_iter` / `BLOCK_STEPS`, the unblocked step budget). It found the real bug below and, on a chain with grafted ridge components, stopping at 33 rounds leaves a log-likelihood gap of 6.8 against 0.033 converged.
  - **Bug: block-wise fits passed their round test short of the fixed point** (main too). On the chained test families in blocks of 300 pairs, at the default *tol*, three units stopped ~0.1 from the whole fit (log-likelihood gap 0.015) after 16 rounds; at *tol* 1e-10 it took 94 rounds to reach it. Blocks were runs of unit ids, so linked units sat in different blocks and each block's fit held their shared k-mers fixed.
  - **Fix (`_fit_blockwise`).**
    - *Blocks in graph order:* runs of units in reverse Cuthill–McKee order of the unit graph (`_unit_order`, scipy), so units sharing k-mers mostly share a block. This alone removes the false convergence (gap 0.015 → 1e-11).
    - *Rounds accelerated as steps are:* SQUAREM over rounds (two rounds, a squared extrapolation, a stabilising round; the step caps and fallback of `_squarem`). With the graph order: 15 rounds against 18 (families), 645 against 999 at the cap (chain + ridges).
    - `MAX_BLOCK_ROUNDS` 1,000 stays as a stop; `BLOCK_STEPS` stays 30 (more steps per block let blocks reach their own fixed points: gap 1.4–9.5 at 100–1,000 steps).

    | Synthetic (log-likelihood gap to the whole fit) | `main` | new |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | families, blocks of 300 | 16 rounds, gap 0.015 | 15 rounds, gap 1e-11 |
    | chain only, blocks of 3,000 | 9 rounds, converged | 9 rounds, converged |
    | chain + 31 ridge components | 300 rounds, gap 0.044 | 645 rounds, gap 0.033 |

    - *Limit:* ridge-heavy block-wise components remain slow, maybe slower than before (more rounds to a better optimum). The chosen tiers do not block at 100 M pairs (`tbase05_all`'s largest component is 5.5 M pairs); raising `MAX_FIT_PAIRS` (unblocked EM peaks ~0.45 GB per M pairs) is the fallback if HPC shows it.
  - **New stat `em_max_change`** (report, `--stats`, `query_cost.tsv` in scientific notation): the largest last change, against *tol*, of a component stopped at the cap (0 if none). Test updates: `test_em_reports_unconverged_components`; new `test_blockwise_em_does_not_stop_short` (fails on main: 0.11 off).
  - **Next:** `hpc/kfp-ablations/em-query-cost`: `tbase05_all` and `tbase10_all` (tiers-novaseq's indexes) at 40 M and 100 M pairs in a fresh run directory; compare `fit_em_wall_s`, `em_*` and the profiles with `tiers-query-cost`.

* **Phase 7, step 30 — EM convergence on deep soil metagenomes, from several starts (results).** Step 29's checks are synthetic or on the fmh benchmark's simulated gut samples. A deeply sequenced, species-rich sample (soil) gives larger components with more units sharing k-mers, and has no truth, so convergence is judged by agreement between fits from different starting points.
  - **Random EM starts** (`query --em-start N`, `profile(em_start=N)`): N > 0 seeds random starting points for every EM fit (plain, zero-inflated, empirical-Bayes, *p\_in*; not the posterior's): coverages times a log-normal factor of sd 1 (`START_SPREAD`), presence or scale uniform on [0.3, 1] (`START_PRESENT_MIN`). Test: `test_random_em_starts_reach_one_fixed_point` (on the test families, three random starts reach the usual start's coverages within 1e-5 for the plain, zero-inflated and *p\_in* fits; measured spread ≤ 1.1 × 10⁻⁶ at the default *tol*).
  - **Workflow (`mgnify-subset --query`):** `query_starts` (default `0`) runs each draws-0 cell once per start; stats and profiles are named `{index}.{pairs}.{draws}[.s{seed}]`, `query_cost.tsv` gains `em_start`, and with several starts `EM_STARTS` writes `em_starts.tsv` (`mgnify_subset.py em-starts`). Over the units gather keeps, per estimate: spread across starts (max − min over the mean for coverages and abundances; max − min for `present_zi` and `aai`), at two levels, by unit and by `component` (summed over hit units linked by shared k-mers), each overall and by `kmers_unique` bin (1–2, 3–9, 10+). Columns: the share within 0.1%, 1% and 10% (by count, and by value for coverages), the largest spread, and the largest total-variation distance of a start's normalised profile from the usual start's. **Units that disagree while their component agrees are unidentifiable splits** (coverage moved between units sharing their k-mers, as in step 29); **components that disagree did not converge**. Tests: `test_em_starts_tells_unidentifiable_splits_from_unconverged_fits`; a real run of the query workflow on the fixture with three starts and `--aai`.
  - **Run** (`hpc/kfp-ablations/em-starts-soil`, after `tiers-novaseq`): two deep soil runs, each profiled alone at 40 M, 100 M and all its pairs, five starts, `--aai`, against tiers-novaseq's `slim`, `tbase02_all` (for the standard tier), `tbase05_all` and `tbase10_all` (whose largest components are fitted block-wise, the path step 29 changed):
    - SRR12659369, Harvard Forest LTER forest soil (PRJNA654925): 178,144,788 pairs, 2 × 151 bp NovaSeq 6000, 24 GB.
    - SRR11613032, Everglades Agricultural Area soil (PRJNA620814): 200,931,322 pairs, 2 × 151 bp NovaSeq 6000, 26 GB.
    - Picked from ENA (soil metagenome, paired Illumina WGS, > 150 M spots); pairs and layout checked with ENA's file report (302 bases per spot, two FASTQ files). 90 queries.
    - *Limit:* the indexes are the 1-in-100 subset, so components are smaller than at full scale; a full-release index will need the same check.
  - **Decision rule.** Converged if, for `coverage_em` and `abundance_zi` in every cell: at component level ≥ 99% of components and ≥ 99.9% of their value within 1%, and `l1_max` ≤ 0.001. Unit-level disagreement confined to `kmers_unique` 1–2 with agreeing components is reported, not a failure (`aai` and `present_zi` for those units are as uncertain). Otherwise, the disagreeing components show which path failed: block-wise (`em_block_rounds` > 0 in the cell's stats) or `em_max_change` > *tol* (stopped at `max_iter`), and the next step is a longer `max_iter` or tighter *tol* on those cells.
  - **Results** (`em-starts-soil` export; main 12b346a; 24 cells (2 runs × 4 indexes × 3 depths) × 5 starts, all tasks completed):
    - *Component level: converged everywhere.* `coverage_em`: 100% of components within 0.1% in every cell and every `kmers_unique` bin; largest spread 8.3 × 10⁻⁴. `abundance_zi`: ≥ 99.997% of components and ≥ 99.915% of value within 1% (lowest: `slim` at 40 M pairs, both runs); largest spread 1.49 (one component, SRR12659369 `tbase02_all` at all pairs). `coverage_zi` is a little looser (≥ 99.97% within 1%, value ≥ 99.77%), but the rule does not use it.
    - *Unit level: splits, mostly in units with few unique k-mers.* `coverage_em` 99.995% of units within 1% (value ≥ 99.37%), `abundance_zi` ≥ 98.9%. Spreads reach 5.0, the maximum for five starts (a unit at zero in four starts), in every `kmers_unique` bin, though the 10+ bin has ≤ 0.012% of units off by 1%. Their components agree, so these are unidentifiable splits. `present_zi` differs by more than 0.01 in 0.04–1.1% of units (up to a full flip, spread 1); `aai` is within 1% for ≥ 99.4%, largest spread 0.089.
    - *`l1_max` (unit profiles, the largest TV distance of a start from the usual start):* ≤ 7.8 × 10⁻⁴ in 21 of 24 cells (≤ 2.1 × 10⁻⁴ outside `tbase10_all`). The misses are all SRR11613032 `tbase10_all`: 1.34 × 10⁻³ (`abundance_zi`, 40 M pairs), 1.01 × 10⁻³ and 1.04 × 10⁻³ (`coverage_em` and `abundance_zi`, 100 M). Only units carry an L1 (components have none), so these misses are splits too.
    - *Fits at the cap.* Almost every cell ran to 1,000 steps. Outside `tbase10_all`, 0–229 k units were still moving (the most in SRR11613032 `tbase02_all` at 100 M and all pairs), with `em_max_change` ≤ 8.7 × 10⁻⁶. SRR11613032 `tbase10_all` at all three depths, and SRR12659369 `tbase10_all` at all pairs, stopped with 0.49–0.78 M units still moving, most of them in a largest component of 1.4–1.8 M units: `em_max_change` up to 1.7 × 10⁻⁵, 1,700× *tol*. These hold the three L1 misses. SRR12659369 `tbase10_all` at 40 M and 100 M converged in 399–666 steps.
    - *Block-wise fits not tested.* The largest component reached 8.6 M pairs (SRR11613032 `tbase10_all`, all pairs), under `MAX_FIT_PAIRS` (10 M), so no cell went block-wise: there is no `em_block_rounds` column. Step 29's change to that path is still unchecked on real data. Single soil runs at the 1-in-100 subset give smaller components than step 27's pooled gut runs (10.9 M).
    - *Cost:* `fit_em` ≤ 109 s and `fit_zi` ≤ 173 s per query (`tbase10_all`, all pairs); peak anonymous memory ≤ 4.4 GB.
  - **Against the rule:** coverage and abundance converge at component level in every cell. `l1_max` ≤ 0.001 fails in 3 of 24 cells by up to 34%, all unit-level splits inside agreeing components, in the one index whose giant component stops at the cap. Read as the rule's exception (unit disagreement with agreeing components is reported, not a failure), **the EM passes**. Read strictly, the next step is the rule's own: rerun SRR11613032 `tbase10_all` with a larger `max_iter`. That only matters if `tbase10_all` ships with unit-level output; its splits are unidentifiable at any `max_iter`.
  - **Decided:** pass, under the exception (user, 2026-10-06). No `max_iter` rerun.
  - **Open:** the block-wise path needs a component above 10 M pairs, either from a pooled soil run or a full-scale index.

* **Phase 7, step 31 — third ablation export: the standard candidates and frame mode on the release tiers (results; no decision taken).** `results/ablations-v3`: the export minus `units/` and `mgnify/` (135 MB; the 14.8 GB tarball does not fit unpacked, so those stay in it). Runs `standard-novaseq`, `frames-novaseq`, `frames-miseq` at main e2dbf27, all tasks completed (OOM retries; two `AAI_SCORE` segfaults, exit 139, passed on retry). `standard-query-cost` has no `em_max_change` column, so it ran the EM from before step 29. `frames-query-cost` came as a separate export (`results/ablations-v3/frames-query-cost`); in it, `all` ran after step 29's EM change and the other modes before it. Index sizes are from the trace's `wchar` of `MGNIFY_INDEX`, which equals step 27's `du` for the indexes measured there. Numbers are means over 10 seeds. MGnify90 detection at matched thresholds was rescored offline, as in step 27 (`rescore.py`: one streaming pass over the tarball, `bench.aai_score` at `kmers_unique` ≥ 1–4; `rescored_aai_scores.tsv`). At ≥ 1 it reproduces the HPC scores exactly in 289 of 290 profiles. The exception is MiSeq seed 9 `slim~edges20`, where the HPC row has 1,707 fewer `genes_with_hit`, a count that depends only on gene units and truth: the same kind of oddity as step 27's `floor-26.4M` seed 7. The rescored row is used.
  - **Standard candidates** (NovaSeq; GB at 1 in 100 as in step 27, ×100 for today's full-scale format; compact estimated by `slim`'s ratio, 50 / 91):

    | Index | GB | Full / compact | `aai` ρ | raw bias 90 / 80 / 70% | calibrated 70% (seeds) | `completeness_90` at `purity_nearest` 0.15 / 0.20 | Pfam at sparse-at-1 purity |
    | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
    | sparse | 0.77 | 77 / 42 | −0.19 | +0.061 / +0.128 / +0.232 | +0.205 | — / 0.235 | 0.742 (≥ 1) |
    | `tbase02_all16` | 1.96 | 196 / 108 | 0.22 | +0.045 / +0.096 / +0.178 | **−0.037** (−0.063 to +0.052) | 0.554 / 0.479 | 0.779 (≥ 2) |
    | `tbase02_n16` | 2.01 | 201 / 111 | 0.26 | +0.042 / +0.088 / +0.166 | **−0.050** (−0.066 to +0.036) | **0.604 / 0.541** | 0.778 (≥ 2) |
    | `tbase015_all` | 2.10 | 210 / 115 | 0.44 | +0.042 / +0.074 / +0.135 | −0.003 (−0.045 to +0.024) | 0.514 / 0.397 | 0.775 (≥ 3) |
    | `tbase02_all` | 2.22 | 222 / 122 | 0.43 | +0.038 / +0.074 / +0.136 | −0.004 (−0.037 to +0.022) | 0.560 / 0.461 | 0.779 (≥ 3) |

    - *`tbase02_n16`'s `aai` failure is the 16-k-mer cap, not selection by score:* `tbase02_all16`, the same cap sampled by hash, misses calibration as well and has a lower Spearman. Step 23's per-unit selection argument does not explain it; too few k-mers per unit does. `tbase02_n16` repeats step 27 (−0.051 then, −0.050 now).
    - *`tbase015_all` is the only candidate below `tbase02_all` that calibrates,* with the same `aai` (ρ 0.44, bias within 0.001) for 5% less disk. It pays in detection: the lowest `completeness_90` at matched purity (singletons drop to 0.015, step 28's expected cost).
    - *Detection:* `tbase02_n16` leads at every purity (+0.04 to +0.08 over `tbase02_all`); `tbase02_all16` ≈ `tbase02_all`.
    - *Pfam:* every candidate at matched purity sits at completeness 0.775–0.779 against sparse's 0.742 (Spearman 0.844–0.848 against 0.799, L1 0.496–0.505 against 0.534). Pfam does not separate them.
    - *Abundance by length:* `abund_err_len0` 0.795–0.841, sparse 0.804. No candidate fixes short genes; frame mode does (below).
    - *Query cost at 100 M pairs:* 223 s (`n16`), 228 s (`015_all`), 341 s (`all16`), 352 s (`tbase02_all`); ≤ 2.1 GB anonymous; EM 15–26 s. The spread is in lookup: 65–178 s, and `tbase02_all` took 178 s here against 66 s in step 27 on the same index. So it is the node's I/O, and query cost does not separate the candidates. `tbase015_all` at 100 M pairs reported 297,824 unconverged units under the pre-step-29 EM.
  - **Frame mode** (`slim`, `tbase02_all`, `tbase05_all`; stop-free, edges20, edges30, all). At `kmers_unique` ≥ 1, stop-free → edges20 → edges30:

    | Read model, index | `completeness_90_len0` | `abund_err_len0` | `abund_bias_len0` | `abund_err_len150` | `completeness_90` at `purity_nearest` 0.20 |
    | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
    | NovaSeq `slim` | 0.223 / 0.239 / 0.238 | 0.846 / 0.702 / 0.729 | −0.31 / +0.03 / −0.04 | 0.748 / 0.725 / 0.712 | 0.278 / 0.276 / 0.277 |
    | NovaSeq `tbase02_all` | 0.437 / 0.479 / 0.473 | 0.800 / 0.770 / 0.768 | −0.27 / +0.12 / +0.05 | 0.742 / 0.705 / 0.711 | 0.461 / 0.471 / 0.469 |
    | NovaSeq `tbase05_all` | 0.580 / 0.635 / 0.628 | 0.814 / 0.708 / 0.703 | −0.32 / +0.08 / −0.01 | 0.712 / 0.687 / 0.692 | 0.637 / 0.654 / 0.651 |
    | MiSeq `slim` | 0.153 / 0.248 / 0.247 | 1.195 / 0.701 / 0.711 | −1.00 / −0.10 / −0.10 | 0.798 / 0.692 / 0.698 | 0.278 / 0.286 / 0.287 |
    | MiSeq `tbase02_all` | 0.308 / 0.497 / 0.493 | 1.203 / 0.724 / 0.729 | −0.98 / +0.10 / +0.05 | 0.776 / 0.660 / 0.663 | 0.459 / 0.493 / 0.493 |
    | MiSeq `tbase05_all` | 0.393 / 0.653 / 0.650 | 1.193 / 0.705 / 0.713 | −1.03 / +0.03 / +0.01 | 0.762 / 0.653 / 0.651 | 0.627 / 0.674 / 0.673 |

    - *The length bias the plan predicted is real on the fmh benchmark, unlike step 7's simulation.* Stop-free halves the abundance of genes under 150 aa on MiSeq (log2 bias −1.0, and +0.28 to +0.37 for genes over 600 aa) and costs NovaSeq −0.3. Edges remove it on both read models and cut `abund_err_len0` by 0.4–0.5 on MiSeq, 0.03–0.14 on NovaSeq. `completeness_90_len0` rises by up to +0.26 on MiSeq.
    - *No purity cost at matched thresholds.* MGnify90: at `purity_nearest` 0.15–0.20, `completeness_90` is higher with edges on `tbase02_all` and `tbase05_all` (+0.01 to +0.02 NovaSeq, +0.03 to +0.05 MiSeq) and equal on `slim`. The exception is `slim` at 0.25, reachable only at ≥ 4 hits with edges, where stop-free leads (0.207 against 0.174 NovaSeq). Pfam: at stop-free's purity at `min_hits` 1 and 2, completeness differs by ≤ 0.004 in every arm, while L1 falls 0.010–0.013 (NovaSeq) and 0.049–0.054 (MiSeq) and Spearman rises 0.003–0.006 and 0.023–0.030. Step 28's purity loss at `min_hits` 1 is the threshold shift, as suspected.
    - *Calibrated `aai` unchanged* (`tbase02_all`, `tbase05_all`: 70% bias within 0.002 of stop-free; raw ρ +0.01 to +0.04).
    - *All six frames gain nothing over edges* (`completeness_90_len0` ≤ +0.006, the same Pfam and errors) at the highest cost.
    - *edges20 against edges30:* edges20 has +0.001 to +0.007 short-gene completeness; abundance error is mixed. edges30 is cheaper.
    - *Profile time (median PROFILE on 6.6 M reads, over stop-free):* NovaSeq edges20 1.13–1.39×, edges30 1.10–1.26×, all 1.27–1.60×; MiSeq edges20 1.29–1.64×, edges30 1.25–1.58×, all 1.49–2.19×. The largest ratios are on `slim`, the smallest on `tbase05_all`, whose lookups dominate.
    - **Query cost** (`frames-query-cost`; ERR7738575 + ERR7746321 pooled; over stop-free at 100 M pairs; `slim` / `tbase02_all` / `tbase05_all`):

      | Mode | k-mers sampled | k-mers hit | wall | CPU | lookup CPU | peak anonymous GB |
      | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
      | stop-free | 2.44 G | 35 / 139 / 170 M | 199 / 230 / 272 s | 619 / 885 / 951 s | 1.0× | 1.2 / 2.0 / 3.0 |
      | edges20 | 1.82× | 1.28 / 1.18 / 1.17× | 1.20 / 1.37 / 1.16× | 1.48 / 1.38 / 1.24× | 1.60–2.02× | 1.5 / 2.3 / 3.4 |
      | edges30 | 1.52× | 1.21 / 1.13 / 1.12× | **1.02** / 1.31 / **1.03**× | 1.27 / 1.55 / 1.15× | 1.35–2.25× | 1.3 / 2.3 / 3.2 |
      | all | 2.29× | 1.34 / 1.21 / 1.20× | 1.10 / 1.43 / 1.28× | 1.73 / 1.74 / 1.38× | 1.85–2.43× | 1.5 / 2.3 / 3.2 |

      - *The workload is deterministic; the times are not.* Hashing CPU barely grows with edges (≤ 1.3×; the reads are decoded once whatever the mode), while lookup scales with the sampled k-mers (edges30 1.5×, edges20 1.8×). Wall time is noisy at the 50% level: `tbase05_all` edges30 at 40 M pairs ran 0.91× stop-free, and `slim` stop-free hashing took 132 s against 83 s for edges20. The EM is ≤ 50 s everywhere; it is not what frame mode costs.
      - `tbase05_all` at 4 M pairs: 210,913 (edges20) and 212,307 (all) unconverged units, under both EM versions, with `em_max_change` 3.7 × 10⁻⁸ (all): its largest component on a flat ridge (step 29), not an error.
    - *Against step 28's rule:* lower `abund_err_len0`/`len150` and higher `completeness_90_len0`/`len150` on both read models: **met** (both edges). No purity loss at matched thresholds: **met**, except `slim` at the highest purity. ≤ 25% more query time at 100 M pairs: edges30 **met on `slim` and `tbase05_all` by wall time** (1.02×, 1.03×) and **missed on `tbase02_all`** (1.31×; the nearest measured index to the chosen standard tier, `tbase02_all16`). By CPU it misses on `slim` (1.27×) and `tbase02_all` (1.55×). Edges20 misses on `tbase02_all` either way. All: rejected (no gain over edges, the highest cost).
  - **What this means (for the user to decide):**
    - *Standard tier:* decided, `tbase02_all16` (project-wide, 2026-10-06).
    - *Frame mode:* decided, `edges:30` (project-wide, 2026-10-06). It meets the rule's accuracy criteria, and its time limit on the slim and large tiers by wall time, but not on the standard-tier stand-in or by CPU; the user accepted that cost.
  - **Next:** (1) `aai-score --model` (step 25) on these profiles, to see whether the selection-conditioned estimate rescues `tbase02_n16`; (2) `em-query-cost` and `em-starts-soil` (steps 29–30) for the EM.

* **Phase 7, step 32 — the three release tiers built at full scale (set up, not run).** `hpc/kfp-ablations/release-tiers`: mgnify-subset's partitioned build over the whole release (`--biome root --sample 1`, 256 shards, buckets and ranges), once per decided tier, into `results-<tier>/index`: `slim` (`--t-base 0.01 --t-base-singleton 0.001 --n-min 8`), standard `tbase02_all16` (`--t-base 0.02 --n-min 16 --oversample 1`) and large `tbase05_all` (`--t-base 0.05 --n-min 32 --oversample 1`), all `--k 11`. The `index` ablation run does not do this: it builds one-factor variants on KO/Pfam units, and `tiers`/`standard` build the tiers at 1 in 100 only.
  - *One launch directory, tiers in turn, `-resume`:* the release extraction (MEMBERSHIP, EXTRACT, MERGE) runs once and the build steps run per tier. CONCAT starts at 128 GB (slim) or 256 GB (standard, large) and BLOOM at 64 GB, since both scale with the floor (phase 6: tier 2 ~31 GB and filter ~14 GB for the 77 GB sparse build).
  - **Bug fixed: `-resume` ignored a changed `--index_args`.** Nextflow (26.04 here) hashes a task's script template, not the values of the `params` it references, so a second tier in the same directory reused the first one's build: on the fixture, standard and large came out with slim's parameters. CANDIDATES and INDEX now take `index_args` as a `val` input; later build steps take CANDIDATES' files, so they rerun too. Checked on the fixture (`test,test_build`): each tier reruns 18 build tasks, reuses 6 extraction tasks, and its `meta.json` holds its own parameters. Other processes that read `params` in their scripts (e.g. QUERY's `query_args`) have the same exposure when a directory is resumed with changed options.
  - *Not included:* the human mask sidecar (`mask`) and the `aai` survival model (`aai-model`, from the aai-model fit) or calibration map (`calibrate-aai`); each attaches to a built index afterwards.

## Libraries

Most of the plumbing exists; the amino-acid k-mer hashing and the translation LUT are small enough to write yourself.

| Need | Crate | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FASTA/FASTQ parsing | `paraseq` or `needletail` | paraseq has parallel paired-end record sets; needletail is the mature default. |
| Compression | `niffler` + `flate2` (zlib-rs backend), `zstd` | Transparent gz/zst input. |
| DNA minimizers/hashing | [`simd-minimizers`](https://github.com/rust-seq/simd-minimizers), `packed-seq`, `seq-hash` | Needs AVX2 or NEON; also accepts ASCII text, so could sample amino-acid strings. |
| General hashing | `xxhash-rust` or `wyhash` | For u64-packed amino-acid k-mers. |
| sourmash compatibility | `sourmash` (Rust core) | Protein/Dayhoff/HP encodings and its hash, for the compat mode. |
| Parallelism | `rayon`, `crossbeam-channel` | Reader → worker → reducer pipeline. |
| Index storage | `memmap2`, `rkyv` or `bincode`; `ptr_hash` or sorted arrays | Zero-copy, memory-mapped index. |
| Stats | `statrs` | Poisson/binomial for coverage correction. |
| CLI, errors, logs | `clap`, `anyhow`/`thiserror`, `tracing`, `indicatif` | Standard. |
| Testing | `proptest`, `insta`, `assert_cmd`, `cargo-nextest` | Invariants, golden outputs, CLI tests. |
| Benchmarks | `criterion` or `divan` | Micro-benchmarks of hashing and translation. |
| Frame calling (optional) | [FragGeneScanRs](https://github.com/unipept/FragGeneScanRs) | GPL-3.0: call as a subprocess or keep your crate GPL. |

For binaries using the SIMD crates, pin `target-cpu=x86-64-v3` in release builds; Groot Koerkamp's [post on distributing SIMD binaries](https://curiouscoding.nl/posts/distributing-rust-simd-binaries/) covers bioconda and the `ensure_simd` check.

**Bridge and Python side (phases 0–5)**

| Need | Library | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Rust ↔ Python | `pyo3`, `maturin`, `numpy` (the crate `rust-numpy`) | Zero-copy numpy outputs from batch kernels; release the GIL inside kernels. |
| Environment and builds | `uv` with the maturin build backend | `uv sync` rebuilds the extension; `maturin develop --release` for fast iteration. |
| MGnify subsets | `duckdb` over MGnify's Parquet files | Remote querying of MGnify Proteins via Parquet/DuckDB is documented by MGnify. |
| Tables and storage | `polars`, `pyarrow`, `numpy` | Parquet for index tables during prototyping. |
| Statistics | `scipy` (optimize, stats), `numpy` | EM, zero-inflated negative binomial, dispersion tests. |
| sourmash compat | `sourmash` Python package | Direct comparison with fmh-funprofiler sketches. |
| CLI | `typer` | Prototype CLI mirroring the planned Rust one. |
| Testing | `pytest`, `hypothesis`, `pytest-benchmark` | Property tests mirror the Rust `proptest` ones. |
| Lint, format, types | `ruff`, `mypy` | Run in pre-commit and CI. |
| Plots, notebooks | `matplotlib`, Jupyter or marimo | Evaluation only; kept out of the package. |

## Benchmark labels

Pfam is the primary label for benchmarking; KO is kept as a secondary label.

- **Why Pfam.** The released index labels MGnify90 clusters with MGnify's own Pfam hits (no KO or eggNOG annotation run), so Pfam is what users will report and what defaults should be chosen on. Pfam is also open, so truth sets and indexes can be shared.
- **Why keep KO.** fmh-funprofiler profiles KEGG orthologs and is the direct predecessor, so a KO benchmark is the only like-for-like comparison with it (the phase-3 gate was passed on KO). KO results are reported beside Pfam results, but decisions and gates from phase 5 on use Pfam.
- **Pfam truth.** Annotate the benchmark genomes' proteins with Pfam-A (`hmmsearch --cut_ga`, the Pfam release of MGnify's `mgy_proteins_pfam`). A Pfam is present if a read overlaps one of its domain hits (the domain's coordinates, not the whole gene), and its depth is Σ over those domains of aligned bases / domain length, as for KOs. Multi-domain proteins count towards each of their Pfams.
- **Our profiles.** Two index types: Pfam units built from the benchmark's reference proteins (the analogue of the KO units, isolating the method from the reference), and MGnify90 cluster indexes where each Pfam's abundance is Σ over clusters carrying it of `coverage_zi` (the shipped configuration).
- **Other tools on Pfam.** DIAMOND: a read counts towards the Pfams whose domains its alignment overlaps on the subject. HUMAnN: UniRef90 families regrouped to Pfam (`humann_regroup_table -g uniref90_pfam`). fmh-funprofiler and kMermaid stay on KO only: fmh-funprofiler ships KO sketches, and kMermaid's disjoint clusters do not fit multi-domain proteins.

## Test and benchmark data

Three tiers: tiny hand-built fixtures in the repo, a small simulated community for CI, and large benchmarks run outside CI.

| Tier | Data | Tests | Where |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Unit fixtures (< 1 MB) | \~20 proteins with their CDS; hand-built reads: in-frame, each of 6 frames, reverse strand, spanning a stop, with synonymous and non-synonymous changes, with N, with an indel. | Translation, frame filter, silent-change invariance, threshold nesting. | `tests/data/`, generated by a script in `scripts/` so they are reproducible. |
| CI community (\~5–10 MB) | 5 complete genomes spanning GC: *S. aureus* (low GC), *E. coli* K-12, *B. subtilis* 168, *P. aeruginosa* PAO1 (high GC), one archaeon. Reads from InSilicoSeq with known origins; truth = per-gene coverage from read origins mapped through annotations. | End-to-end golden output (insta snapshot), GC effect on frame filter, abundance error bounds. | Repo via Git LFS, or Zenodo with a checksum-verified download step. |
| fmh-funprofiler datasets | Their simulated metagenomes and KO ground truth, plus Pfam truth from the same genomes. | Head-to-head at equal density; compat-mode parity (KO); primary accuracy (Pfam). | Zenodo record from their repo. |
| Divergence ladder | Hold out the source genomes' proteins from the index, keeping relatives at \~95/90/80/70% amino-acid identity. | Sensitivity decay vs alphabet, k, spaced seeds. The key experiment for any amino-acid k-mer method. | Built by the eval pipeline. |
| CAMI II (marine, strain madness, plant-associated) | Public simulated metagenomes with genome-level truth. | Functional truth derived by annotating the source genomes with Pfam (primary) and KofamScan KOs (for fmh-funprofiler) and projecting read origins. | CAMI data portal. |
| Mock communities | ZymoBIOMICS standards (e.g. the gut standard) with published reference genomes. | Real sequencing error and library bias with known composition. | ENA/SRA runs. |
| Real cohort | A subset of HMP2/IBDMDB with published HUMAnN outputs. | Concordance with HUMAnN; runtime at scale. | IBDMDB portal. |
| Negative controls | Shuffled reads, human reads, intergenic-only simulated reads. | False-positive rate; human reads also with and without the human mask. | Generated. |
| Host spike-in ladder | fmh benchmark metagenomes plus simulated T2T-CHM13 reads at 0/50/90/99% host. | Host handling: none, upstream removal, mask, decoy (Additional references). | Generated (InSilicoSeq). |

**Reference databases:** develop on a human-gut biome subset of MGnify Proteins 2026\_07; release on the full set (CC0, so pre-built indexes can be shared). Ground-truth genomes for the CI community and CAMI must be annotated against the same unit definitions, e.g. by mapping their predicted proteins to MGnify clusters, and with the same Pfam release as MGnify's labels.

## Repo setup

One GitHub repo holding a uv-managed Python package built with maturin and a Cargo workspace; pre-commit runs fast checks for both languages, and CI tests both plus Rust-vs-Python parity.

**Create**

Done in phase 0 (repo `timrozday-mgnify/kmer-functional-profiler`, package `kmer_functional_profiler`, GPL-3.0-or-later). `uv init --build-backend maturin` was not used: it puts a single crate at the root, which conflicts with the workspace layout below. Instead `pyproject.toml` points maturin at `crates/py` (`manifest-path`, `python-source = "python"`, `module-name = "kmer_functional_profiler._core"`). Dependencies are added in the phase that first uses them (polars, pyarrow, duckdb in phase 2; scipy in phase 4; sourmash in phase 3; pytest-benchmark and rust-numpy in phase 1).

**Layout**

```text
pyproject.toml        # maturin backend; module <name>._core
uv.lock
Cargo.toml            # workspace, shared deps, lints, release profile
rust-toolchain.toml   # pinned stable
.cargo/config.toml    # target-cpu=x86-64-v3 for release
crates/core/          # kernels: fastx, translation, k-mers, hashing, samplers, lookup
crates/py/            # PyO3 bindings over core -> <name>._core
crates/cli/           # phase 8: final Rust binary (index, query, model)
python/<name>/        # prototype: index build, query, model, typer CLI
python/<name>/reference/  # slow pure-Python twins of the kernels (test oracles)
tests/python/  tests/data/  tests/golden/   # golden outputs from the prototype
scripts/              # fixture generation, data download
eval/                 # Nextflow benchmarks, notebooks
docs/spec.md          # algorithm spec, written at end of phase 7
```

Keeping `crates/core` free of PyO3 means phases 6 and 8 reuse the kernels unchanged; only `crates/py` knows about Python.

**Pre-commit** (the `pre-commit` framework, or its Rust port `prek`):

- `pre-commit-hooks`: trailing-whitespace, end-of-file-fixer, check-yaml, check-toml, check-added-large-files (limit 1 MB, so data goes to LFS/Zenodo).
- Python: `ruff check --fix` and `ruff format`; `mypy` on `python/` at the pre-push stage.
- Rust: `cargo fmt --all -- --check`; `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` at the pre-push stage.
- `uv lock --check` so the lockfile never drifts from `pyproject.toml`.
- `typos` for spelling, `taplo` for TOML formatting.

**CI (GitHub Actions)**

- `ci.yml` on push and pull request, three jobs:
  - **rust**: fmt, clippy with `-D warnings`, `cargo nextest`, `cargo doc`, MSRV build, `cargo deny`.
  - **python**: `astral-sh/setup-uv`, `uv sync` (builds the extension in release mode), ruff, mypy, pytest with hypothesis, on Python 3.11–3.13.
  - **parity**: Rust kernels vs `reference/` twins on fixtures and the CI community; from phase 8, the Rust CLI vs `tests/golden/`.
  - Matrix: ubuntu-latest and macos-latest (arm64, covers NEON). Coverage from `pytest-cov` and `cargo-llvm-cov` to Codecov.
- Use `dtolnay/rust-toolchain`, `Swatinem/rust-cache`, and `taiki-e/install-action` for nextest, cargo-deny and llvm-cov.
- `bench.yml`: manual or weekly `pytest-benchmark` and criterion runs; not a merge gate.
- `release.yml` (phase 9): `cargo-dist` binaries on tags, optional wheels via `PyO3/maturin-action`; bioconda recipe after the first tagged release.
- Dependabot for Cargo, uv and Actions; branch protection on `main` requiring `ci.yml`.

## Additional references (study proteins, hosts, contaminants)

Users need three kinds of extra reference beside MGnify90. **Augment:** a study's own proteins (MAG gene predictions, assembly gene calls and their annotations) are the closest references its reads will ever have, and much of a new study's protein content is not yet in MGnify. **Subtract:** host reads (human, mouse, plant), mitochondria and PhiX spike-in should not register as microbial functions. **Compete:** contaminants that only exist as proteins (diet plants, chloroplasts, fungi) should absorb the reads that match them rather than leave those reads to their nearest microbial relatives. The plan handles all three with two query-time mechanisms: querying several indexes jointly, and a mask sidecar. Both work on a released index without rebuilding it. Index overlays and compaction (the earlier "Extending the reference" design) become a conditional last step, built only if joint queries prove too approximate.

### Can several indexes be queried together?

Yes, and almost nothing breaks, because the model links units at query time. Gather and EM find components from the k-mers that actually got hits (`_unit_components` over the hit table), not from build-time components. If the hit tables of several indexes are joined with offset unit ids, a study unit and an MGnify unit that share hit k-mers fall into one component and split those hits in the EM. Units of different indexes that share no hits never interact, so the base profile is unchanged.

| Concern | Effect | Handling |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hash scheme (k, alphabet, hash, seed) | Units are comparable only if their k-mers are hashed the same way. | Required to match, checked from `meta.json`. One read pass serves every index; the query samples at the largest *t\_max*, and each index looks up only hashes ≤ its own *t\_max*. |
| `n_groups` (score, promiscuity cut, floor choice) | Each build counts only its own units. A k-mer held by 40 base units and 40 study units passes both cuts although it is promiscuous across the union. | Accepted as an approximation. The effect is somewhat larger components and slightly wrong scores; the evaluation measures it. An overlay build (step 5) fixes it by probing the base. |
| Duplicate units (a study protein already in an MGnify90 cluster) | Two units with near-identical k-mer sets split the coverage, so each shows about half. Pfam totals are unaffected when the labels agree. | Ambiguity groups report such pairs (computed from the point fit since draws were dropped, 2026-10-02). Assigning study proteins to existing clusters (step 5) removes the duplicates. |
| Fingerprint false hits | Each index adds its own background, Q·ε·*m\_g*/n. | Background computed per index, as now. |
| Unit ids and labels | Unit ids clash across indexes. | Offset ids at load; the output gains an `index` column; each index carries its own label tables. |
| Normalisation (single-copy markers) | Markers come from the base. | Computed from the base only. |
| Cost | Lookups and residency add up across indexes; hashing does not. | A study index (~10⁶ proteins) is MB to GB, negligible next to the base. |

Indexes built with different k or alphabets cannot be joined: their units share no k-mer space and so cannot compete. They can still be queried in one read pass, but their profiles stay separate and may double-count reads.

### Can an index be updated in place?

It can, but it is I/O-heavy. Adding units costs about as much I/O as compaction, and much less than a rebuild; changing existing units is the expensive case.

- *Adding units* (new clusters): their postings spread uniformly over hash space. Tier 2 is packed by hash range, so almost every range changes, which amounts to one sequential rewrite of tier 2 and the dense tier (~50 GB: minutes on HPC). Set ids deduplicated by set hash must be re-resolved for the k-mers that change.
- *Changing existing units* (members added to an MGnify90 cluster): *p\_in* and the floor's choice of kept k-mers need every member's k-mers, and so the cluster's member sequences from the 1 TB release. That cost is high, which is why overlays keep the old *p\_in*.
- *Global ripple:* a new k-mer raises `n_groups` for every unit that holds it, which changes scores, the promiscuity cut, and other units' floors. A strictly exact update recomputes all affected units, which in practice means a partitioned rebuild of those components.
- *Removing* (masks): dropping postings needs the same tier-2 rewrite. A sidecar of masked hashes avoids it.

So the index is treated like an LSM tree: an immutable base, small layers next to it (study indexes, decoy indexes, masks), and an occasional `compact` that merges them into a new base in one sequential pass. Layers come first because they cost nothing to the base and are easy to drop.

### Options compared

| Option | Kind | Handles | Cost | Weakness |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Upstream read removal (hostile or KneadData: bowtie2 or minimap2 against T2T-CHM13) | Subtract, per read | Host, PhiX | One alignment pass, but host-rich samples then hash 10–100× fewer reads | Needs the host genome. A sparse sketch cannot classify single reads, so read-level removal must happen upstream; this tool cannot replace it. |
| Mask sidecar (host genome k-mers ∩ index) | Subtract, per k-mer | Host coding and non-coding reads, mitochondria, PhiX; also flags host-derived MGnify clusters | Build: one six-frame pass over the genome and one merge with tier 2. Query: one `isin` on sampled hashes | Drops microbial k-mers that coincide with host ones: ~10⁻⁵ of postings by chance at 20 letters and k = 11, more where proteins are truly conserved. At Murphy-10 and k = 11, ~2% by chance: use a proteome-only mask there. |
| Decoy index (host or contaminant proteins) queried jointly | Compete | Coding reads of anything with a proteome (diet, chloroplast, fungi, a host without a genome) | A small index plus joint query | Catches only coding reads; host reads are ~98% non-coding. |
| Study index queried jointly | Augment | MAGs, assembly gene calls | A small index plus joint query; no new code beyond step 1 | Duplicate units and `n_groups` approximations (table above) |
| Overlay plus `compact` (old phase-10 design) | Augment | Same, exactly | Cluster assignment by alignment; compaction rewrites tier 2 | Most code; build only if joint queries fall short |
| Rebuild a subset index including the study proteins | Augment | Biome subsets, development | A subset build (laptop to one node) | Not for the full release |
| Post-hoc decontamination with negative controls (e.g. decontam on unit tables) | Subtract, statistical | Kit and reagent bacteria, which have no separate reference because they are in MGnify | None for the tool | Needs control samples; out of scope, but the output tables should stay usable for it |

Recommended defaults:

- **Host-rich samples** (biopsy, skin, oral, sputum): upstream removal in the Nextflow module, plus the human mask as a safety net.
- **Everything else:** the human mask alone. It costs ~10⁻⁵ of postings and removes the ~0.3% per-k-mer chance hits of residual human reads.
- **Decoys and study indexes:** opt-in.

**Why the mask beats a host decoy for genomes.**

- *Coverage of host reads.* The mask is built from the six-frame translation of the whole host genome, so it covers non-coding reads too: repeats, and the low-complexity peptides from translated LINE/Alu.
- *Exactness.* It removes host-derived hits outright, instead of relying on the EM to give them to a decoy. Host variation leaves ~3% of host 11-mers off-reference (heterozygosity ~10⁻³/bp over 33 bp). Those k-mers hit the index at the ~0.3% chance rate, so ~10⁻⁴ of host k-mers still hit by chance.
- *Flagging contaminated clusters.* The per-unit masked fraction flags MGnify90 clusters that come from host contigs gene-called as prokaryotes, a contamination of MGnify itself that no decoy would expose. Such units are reported as `host_like` rather than dropped.

### Implementation plan

Steps 1–2 are small Python changes needed for the phase-7 host ablation, because the result decides whether the release ships a human mask by default. The phase-8 spec and the Rust query must then include them. Steps 3–5 stay in phase 10.

1. **Joint query (`query --extra-index DIR`, repeatable).**
   - `Index.load` each index; error unless k, alphabet, hash scheme and seed match.
   - Sample at the largest *t\_max*; look each hash up in every index whose *t\_max* admits it.
   - Offset unit ids by the cumulative unit counts and join the unit tables (`m`, `pin_sum`, `m_dense`, `pin_sum_dense`, `max_hash_g`, labels) with an `index` column.
   - Leave gather, EM and presence untouched: they already work on the joined hit table. The dense second pass runs per index.
   - Tests:
     - querying [base] and [base, unrelated] gives identical rows for base units;
